(e) A sequence converges to $c$ in the mean square if $\lim_n\mathbb{E}[(X_n-c)^2]=0$; use Markov to show mean-square convergence implies convergence in probability. (f) Show the converse fails.
    Solution

    (a) Means and variances. Both variables take exactly two values, so every expectation is a two-term sum.

    $$\mathbb{E}[X_n]=0\cdot\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)+1\cdot\frac1n=\frac1n .$$

    For the variance use the definition $\operatorname{var}=\mathbb{E}[(X-\mathbb{E}[X])^2]$, i.e. sum $(\text{value}-\text{mean})^2\times\text{probability}$. [Gap filled: the official solution skips the algebra to $(n-1)/n^2$.] $$\operatorname{var}(X_n)=\Big(0-\frac1n\Big)^2\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)+\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)^2\frac1n =\frac{1}{n^2}\cdot\frac{n-1}{n}+\frac{(n-1)^2}{n^2}\cdot\frac1n =\frac{n-1}{n^3}+\frac{(n-1)^2}{n^3}=\frac{(n-1)\big[1+(n-1)\big]}{n^3}=\frac{(n-1)n}{n^3}=\frac{n-1}{n^2}.$$ (Cross-check with $\operatorname{var}=\mathbb{E}[X_n^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X_n])^2$: since $X_n^2=X_n$, this is $\frac1n-\frac1{n^2}=\frac{n-1}{n^2}$ ✓. Verified symbolically for $n=2,5,37$ in G_varX_check_n*.) $$\mathbb{E}[Y_n]=0\cdot\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)+n\cdot\frac1n=1\qquad\text{for every }n .$$ $$\operatorname{var}(Y_n)=(0-1)^2\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)+(n-1)^2\cdot\frac1n=1-\frac1n+\frac{n^2-2n+1}{n}=1-\frac1n+n-2+\frac1n=n-1 .$$ [Gap filled: same omission in the source.] (Cross-check with $\operatorname{var}=\mathbb{E}[Y_n^2]-(\mathbb{E}[Y_n])^2$: since $\mathbb{E}[Y_n^2]=n^2\cdot\frac1n=n$ and $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]=1$, this is $n-(1)^2=n-1$ ✓; verified in G_varY_check_n*.) Sample values: $\operatorname{var}(X_{100})=99/10000=0.0099$, $\operatorname{var}(Y_{100})=99$.

    (b) What Chebyshev says. For $X_n$, centered at its own mean $1/n$: $$\mathbf{P}\Big(\Big|X_n-\frac1n\Big|\ge\epsilon\Big)\le\frac{\operatorname{var}(X_n)}{\epsilon^2}=\frac{n-1}{n^2\epsilon^2}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0,$$ since $(n-1)/n^2\approx 1/n\to 0$. Numerically at $\epsilon=0.1$ the bound is $9.0$ (vacuous) at $n=10$, $0.99$ at $n=100$, $0.0999$ at $n=1000$ (K_Xn_bound_*). So $X_n-\frac1n\to 0$ in probability. [Gap filled: the official solution then jumps to "$X_n\to 0$ in probability" without argument.] Here is the argument, an instance of the Property in §1.5 with $\mu_n=1/n\to 0$. Fix $\epsilon>0$. If $|X_n|\ge\epsilon$ then, by the triangle inequality $|X_n|\le|X_n-\frac1n|+\frac1n$, we must have $|X_n-\frac1n|\ge\epsilon-\frac1n$. For $n>2/\epsilon$ we have $\epsilon-\frac1n>\epsilon/2$, so $$\mathbf{P}(|X_n|\ge\epsilon)\le\mathbf{P}\Big(\Big|X_n-\frac1n\Big|\ge\frac\epsilon2\Big)\le\frac{4(n-1)}{n^2\epsilon^2}\to 0 .$$ Hence $X_n\to 0$ in probability. (Directly from the PMF this is obvious: $\mathbf{P}(|X_n|\ge\epsilon)=\mathbf{P}(X_n=1)=1/n$ for $0<\epsilon\le 1$.)

    For $Y_n$, Chebyshev gives $$\mathbf{P}\big(|Y_n-1|\ge\epsilon\big)\le\frac{\operatorname{var}(Y_n)}{\epsilon^2}=\frac{n-1}{\epsilon^2}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\infty .$$ The bound blows up: at $n=100$, $\epsilon=1$ it is $99$. A bound of $99$ on a probability is worthless. Conclusion: Chebyshev tells us nothing at all about $Y_n$. Not "$Y_n$ diverges" — merely that this particular tool is silent.

    (c) Does $Y_n$ converge in probability? Yes, to $0$ — and we find out by abandoning Chebyshev and reading the PMF. [Gap filled: the official solution asserts $\mathbf{P}(|Y_n|\ge\epsilon)\le 1/n$ without saying where it comes from.] Fix $\epsilon>0$. $Y_n$ takes only the two values $0$ and $n$. The value $0$ satisfies $|0|<\epsilon$, so it never contributes; the value $n$ satisfies $|n|\ge\epsilon$ as soon as $n\ge\epsilon$. Therefore, for every $n\ge\epsilon$, $$\mathbf{P}\big(|Y_n-0|\ge\epsilon\big)=\mathbf{P}(Y_n=n)=\frac1n\ \longrightarrow\ 0 .$$ Since this holds for every $\epsilon>0$, $Y_n\to 0$ in probability. Note the contrast with (b): the exact calculation succeeds precisely where the variance-based bound fails, because the variance is inflated by a value that is enormous but almost never taken.

    (d) Does convergence in probability force $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]\to a$? No — and $Y_n$ itself is the counterexample. It converges in probability to $a=0$ by part (c), yet $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]=1$ for every $n$ by part (a), so $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]\to 1\ne 0$. The reason, again: the escaping value $n$ has probability $1/n$, so its contribution to the mean is $n\times\frac1n=1$, a constant, even as its probability vanishes.

    (e) Mean square $\Rightarrow$ probability. Suppose $\mathbb{E}[(X_n-c)^2]\to 0$. Fix $\epsilon>0$. The random variable $W_n=(X_n-c)^2$ is nonnegative, so Markov applies to it at level $a=\epsilon^2>0$: $$\mathbf{P}\big(|X_n-c|\ge\epsilon\big)\overset{(1)}{=}\mathbf{P}\big((X_n-c)^2\ge\epsilon^2\big)\overset{(2)}{\le}\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[(X_n-c)^2\big]}{\epsilon^2}.$$ Step (1) is the equivalence of the events, since squaring is increasing on $[0,\infty)$ and both $|X_n-c|$ and $\epsilon$ are nonnegative; step (2) is Markov. [SOURCE TYPO — corrected: the printed solution writes the denominator as $c^2$; it must be $\epsilon^2$, since Markov's level is $\epsilon^2$. With $c^2$ the statement would be false — take $c$ huge and the "bound" would be vacuous, or $c=0$ and it would be undefined.] [Gap filled: the source takes the limit without naming the hypothesis.] Now let $n\to\infty$: by hypothesis the numerator $\mathbb{E}[(X_n-c)^2]\to 0$ while $\epsilon^2$ is a fixed positive constant, so the right side tends to $0$; squeezing between $0$ and it, $$\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(|X_n-c|\ge\epsilon\big)=0 .$$ As $\epsilon>0$ was arbitrary, $X_n\to c$ in probability. $\blacksquare$ (Observe this is a strict generalization of the WLLN proof: put $X_n=M_n$, $c=\mu$, and $\mathbb{E}[(M_n-\mu)^2]=\sigma^2/n\to 0$.)

    (f) Probability $\not\Rightarrow$ mean square. Use $Y_n$ once more. It converges to $0$ in probability by (c), but $$\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_n-0)^2\big]=0^2\cdot\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)+n^2\cdot\frac1n=n\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\ \infty ,$$ which is as far from $0$ as possible. So $Y_n$ does not converge to $0$ in the mean square, and the implication in (e) cannot be reversed. The hierarchy is therefore strict: mean square $\Rightarrow$ in probability, with no arrow back.

    Example 1.11 (rec20 P3 — three transformed i.i.d. uniform sequences). $X$ is uniform on $[-1,1]$ and $X_1,X_2,\dots$ are i.i.d. copies of $X$. Which of the following converge in probability, and to what? (a) $X_i$; (b) $Y_i=X_i/i$; (c) $Z_i=(X_i)^i$.
    Solution

    Setup. For $X\sim\text{uniform}[-1,1]$ the density is $f_X(x)=1/2$ on $[-1,1]$, so for $0\le t\le 1$ $$\mathbf{P}(X>t)=\int_t^{1}\tfrac12\,dx=\tfrac{1-t}{2},\qquad \mathbf{P}(X<-t)=\tfrac{1-t}{2},\qquad \mathbf{P}(|X|>t)=1-t .$$ Also $\mathbb{E}[X]=0$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=\frac{(1-(-1))^2}{12}=\frac{4}{12}=\frac13$ (H_var_unif11).

    (a) $X_i$ does not converge in probability. [Gap filled: the source gives only the bare observation that the distribution does not change with $i$.] Here is the argument. Suppose, for contradiction, $X_i\to a$ in probability for some number $a$. Take $\epsilon=1/2$. Because every $X_i$ has the same distribution as $X$, the probability $\mathbf{P}(|X_i-a|\ge 1/2)=\mathbf{P}(|X-a|\ge 1/2)$ does not depend on $i$ at all; call it $q$. Convergence in probability would require this constant sequence to tend to $0$, i.e. $q=0$, i.e. $\mathbf{P}(|X-a|<1/2)=1$: all of the mass of $X$ would have to lie in an interval of length $1$. But $X$ is uniform on an interval of length $2$, so any interval of length $1$ captures probability at most $1/2<1$. Contradiction. Hence no limit exists. Concretely, $\mathbf{P}(|X_i|>0.5)=0.5$ for every $i$ (H_Xi_tail) — nothing concentrates anywhere.

    (b) $Y_i=X_i/i\to 0$ in probability. Fix $\epsilon>0$. Then $$\mathbf{P}\big(|Y_i-0|>\epsilon\big)=\mathbf{P}\Big(\Big|\frac{X_i}{i}\Big|>\epsilon\Big)=\mathbf{P}\big(|X_i|>i\epsilon\big)=\mathbf{P}(X_i>i\epsilon)+\mathbf{P}(X_i<-i\epsilon),$$ the last step because the two events are disjoint. [Gap filled: the source stops here and asserts the limit is 0.] Evaluate: for $i\epsilon\le 1$ each term is $\frac{1-i\epsilon}{2}$, so the sum is $1-i\epsilon$; for $i\epsilon>1$ the event $|X_i|>i\epsilon$ is impossible because $|X_i|\le 1$ always, so the probability is exactly $0$. Therefore $$\mathbf{P}\big(|Y_i|>\epsilon\big)=\max\{0,\,1-i\epsilon\}=0\qquad\text{for every }i>1/\epsilon,$$ which certainly tends to $0$. At $\epsilon=0.5$: the values are $0.5$ at $i=1$ and exactly $0$ from $i=2$ onward (H_Yi_tail_i1, H_Yi_zero_from). So $Y_i\to 0$ in probability — in fact the tail probability does not merely shrink, it becomes identically zero.

    (c) $Z_i=(X_i)^i\to 0$ in probability. Fix $\epsilon$ with $0<\epsilon<1$ (for $\epsilon\ge 1$ the event $|Z_i|>\epsilon$ is impossible since $|X_i|\le 1$ implies $|Z_i|\le 1$, so the probability is $0$ and there is nothing to prove). [SOURCE TYPO — corrected: the printed solution splits $\{|(X_i)^i|>\epsilon\}$ into $\{X_i>\epsilon^{1/i}\}\cup\{X_i<-\epsilon^{1/i}\}$ as though $(X_i)^i$ could always be negative, which is wrong for even $i$. The correct route uses absolute values, and the final number is unaffected.] Since $|Z_i|=|X_i|^i$ and $t\mapsto t^{1/i}$ is increasing on $[0,\infty)$, $$|Z_i|>\epsilon\iff |X_i|^i>\epsilon\iff |X_i|>\epsilon^{1/i},$$ and $0<\epsilon^{1/i}<1$, so we may use the uniform tail formula: $$\mathbf{P}\big(|Z_i|>\epsilon\big)=\mathbf{P}\big(|X_i|>\epsilon^{1/i}\big)=\underbrace{\tfrac12\big(1-\epsilon^{1/i}\big)}_{X_i>\epsilon^{1/i}}+\underbrace{\tfrac12\big(1-\epsilon^{1/i}\big)}_{X_i<-\epsilon^{1/i}}=1-\epsilon^{1/i}=1-\sqrt[i]{\epsilon}.$$ [Gap filled: the source uses this uniform-CDF step without deriving it, and does not justify $\epsilon^{1/i}\to 1$.] Finally, $\epsilon^{1/i}=e^{(\ln\epsilon)/i}$ and $(\ln\epsilon)/i\to 0$ as $i\to\infty$ (a fixed number over $i$), so by continuity of the exponential $\epsilon^{1/i}\to e^{0}=1$ and hence $$\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\big(|Z_i|>\epsilon\big)=1-1=0 .$$ So $Z_i\to 0$ in probability. Numerically at $\epsilon=0.5$: $1-\sqrt[i]{0.5}$ equals $0.5$, $0.2929$, $0.1294$, $0.03406$, $0.006908$, $0.0006929$ at $i=1,2,5,20,100,1000$ (H_Zi_tail_i*) — the decay is like $(\ln(1/\epsilon))/i$, i.e. of order $1/i$.

    The pattern. Each part is decided by an exact computation from the uniform CDF; no inequality is needed anywhere. That is the practical lesson: when the distribution of $Y_n$ is known, compute $\mathbf{P}(|Y_n-a|\ge\epsilon)$ directly, and reserve Chebyshev for when it is not.

    Practice 1.12 (B&T Problem 5.5(c)). With $X_1,X_2,\dots$ i.i.d. uniform on $[-1,1]$, let $W_n=X_1X_2\cdots X_n$. Show $W_n\to 0$ in probability. (Hint: Markov on $|W_n|$.)
    Solution

    $|W_n|=|X_1||X_2|\cdots|X_n|$ is nonnegative, and the $|X_i|$ are independent, so expectation factorizes: $$\mathbb{E}\big[|W_n|\big]=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[|X_i|\big]=\Big(\mathbb{E}|X|\Big)^n .$$ For $X$ uniform on $[-1,1]$, $\mathbb{E}|X|=\int_{-1}^{1}|x|\tfrac12\,dx=2\int_0^1 x\cdot\tfrac12\,dx=\big[\tfrac{x^2}{2}\big]_0^1=\tfrac12$ (L_EabsX_unif11). Hence $\mathbb{E}|W_n|=(1/2)^n$. Now Markov, applied to the nonnegative $|W_n|$ at level $\epsilon>0$: $$\mathbf{P}\big(|W_n-0|\ge\epsilon\big)=\mathbf{P}\big(|W_n|\ge\epsilon\big)\le\frac{\mathbb{E}|W_n|}{\epsilon}=\frac{(1/2)^n}{\epsilon}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0,$$ since $(1/2)^n\to 0$ geometrically and $\epsilon$ is fixed. Therefore $W_n\to 0$ in probability. At $\epsilon=0.1$ the bound is $5.0$ (vacuous) at $n=1$, $0.3125$ at $n=5$, $0.009766$ at $n=10$, $9.54\times 10^{-6}$ at $n=20$ (L_prod_markov_n*), first dropping below $1$ at $n=4$ (L_prod_first_useful_n).

    Practice 1.13 (B&T Problem 5.5(d)). Same $X_i$; let $V_n=\max\{X_1,\dots,X_n\}$. Does $V_n$ converge in probability, and to what?
    Solution

    Guess the limit $1$, the top of the range. Fix $0<\epsilon<2$. Since $V_n\le 1$ always, $|V_n-1|>\epsilon$ means $V_n<1-\epsilon$, i.e. every $X_i$ is below $1-\epsilon$. By independence and the uniform CDF $\mathbf{P}(X_i<t)=\frac{t+1}{2}$ for $t\in[-1,1]$, $$\mathbf{P}\big(|V_n-1|>\epsilon\big)=\mathbf{P}(X_1<1-\epsilon)\cdots\mathbf{P}(X_n<1-\epsilon)=\left(\frac{(1-\epsilon)+1}{2}\right)^{\!n}=\Big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\Big)^{\!n}.$$ Since $0<1-\epsilon/2<1$, this geometric sequence tends to $0$, so $V_n\to 1$ in probability. At $\epsilon=0.1$ the values $0.95^n$ are $0.95$, $0.5987$, $0.07694$, $0.005921$ at $n=1,10,50,100$ (L_max_tail_n*), and $n\ge 90$ is needed to push the probability below $0.01$ (L_max_n_for_001). Contrast this with Example 1.7: minima of uniforms slide to the bottom of the range, maxima to the top, and both do so in probability at a geometric rate.

    §1 cheatsheet.
    • Markov ($X\ge 0$, $a>0$): $\ \mathbf{P}(X\ge a)\le\dfrac{\mathbb{E}[X]}{a}$. Proof: $X\ge a\mathbf{1}_{\{X\ge a\}}$, take expectations. Vacuous when $a\le\mathbb{E}[X]$. Attained by the two-point variable $\{0,a\}$.
    • Chebyshev (any $X$ with mean $\mu$, variance $\sigma^2$, $c>0$): $\ \mathbf{P}(|X-\mu|\ge c)\le\dfrac{\sigma^2}{c^2}$, equivalently $\mathbf{P}(|X-\mu|\ge k\sigma)\le 1/k^2$. Proof: Markov on $(X-\mu)^2$ at level $c^2$. Vacuous for $k\le 1$. Attained by the three-point variable $\{-k,0,k\}$ with masses $\frac1{2k^2},1-\frac1{k^2},\frac1{2k^2}$.
    • Unknown variance: if $X\in[a,b]$ then $\sigma^2\le\dfrac{(b-a)^2}{4}$; for $X\in\{0,1\}$ this is $\operatorname{var}=p(1-p)\le 1/4$, attained at $p=1/2$.
    • Sample mean ($X_i$ i.i.d., mean $\mu$, variance $\sigma^2$): $\ \mathbb{E}[M_n]=\mu$ (linearity only), $\operatorname{var}(M_n)=\sigma^2/n$ (independence used once), $\sigma_{M_n}=\sigma/\sqrt n$.
    • WLLN: $\ \mathbf{P}(|M_n-\mu|\ge\epsilon)\le\dfrac{\sigma^2}{n\epsilon^2}\to 0$ for each fixed $\epsilon>0$; hence $M_n\to\mu$ in probability. Interprets empirical frequency as an estimate of probability.
    • Sample size: $\ \mathbf{P}(|M_n-\mu|\ge\epsilon)\le\delta$ is guaranteed by $n\ge\dfrac{\sigma^2}{\epsilon^2\delta}$. Bernoulli worst case: $n\ge\dfrac{1}{4\epsilon^2\delta}$. Pollster with $\epsilon=0.01,\delta=0.05$: $n=50{,}000$ (CLT would need $9{,}604$). Accuracy costs $1/\epsilon^2$; confidence costs only $1/\delta$.
    • Convergence in probability: $Y_n\to a$ iff $\mathbf{P}(|Y_n-a|\ge\epsilon)\to 0$ for every $\epsilon>0$. Does not require independence; does not imply $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]\to a$; does not imply mean-square convergence; says nothing about any single realized run.
    • Sufficient test: $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]\to\mu$ and $\operatorname{var}(Y_n)\to 0\ \Rightarrow\ Y_n\to\mu$ in probability. Mean square $\Rightarrow$ in probability (Markov on $(Y_n-c)^2$), never the converse.
    • The canonical counterexample: $\mathbf{P}(Y_n=0)=1-\frac1n$, $\mathbf{P}(Y_n=n)=\frac1n$. Then $Y_n\to 0$ in probability, $\mathbb{E}[Y_n]=1$, $\operatorname{var}(Y_n)=n-1$, $\mathbb{E}[Y_n^2]=n\to\infty$, and Chebyshev is useless.
    • Method choice: known distribution → exact; sample mean and want a number → CLT (§2); know $\mu,\sigma^2$ → Chebyshev; know only $\mathbb{E}[X]$ with $X\ge 0$ → Markov (Fig. 1.3).

    §2   The Central Limit Theorem

    Sources: L20 slides 1–8 (raster p01–p02) · rec21 P1, P2, P3 (with the official solutions, every [SOLUTION GAP] filled and both [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE]s solved) · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis §5.4 "The Central Limit Theorem" (pp. 273–279, its Figure 5.3), including B&T Examples 5.9–5.12 and B&T Problem 5.10 (p. 290). Every number on this page is recomputed in computes/g6_s2.py (167 keys, JSON at computes/g6_s2.json); every figure is generated by computes/g6_s2_figs.py.

    Section 1 gave you inequalities. Markov and Chebyshev are honest: they are guaranteed upper bounds, valid for every distribution with the stated moments, and the weak law of large numbers falls out of Chebyshev in three lines. The price of that honesty is that the bounds are loose — often by one or two orders of magnitude. This section buys precision by giving up the guarantee.

    The trade is worth making because of a fact that has no business being true. Take any distribution with a finite variance — discrete, continuous, symmetric, skewed, it does not matter — add up $n$ independent copies, recenter and rescale, and the resulting distribution stops depending on what you started with. It converges to one universal shape, the standard normal. That is the central limit theorem (CLT), and it is why the bell curve is everywhere.

    Interpretation (where this sits between §1 and §3). All three of §1's results are statements about the sum $S_n=X_1+\dots+X_n$:
    • Markov / Chebyshev bound $\mathbf{P}(|S_n-n\mathbb{E}[X]|\ge a)$ crudely, using one or two moments.
    • The weak law says the deviation $S_n-n\mathbb{E}[X]$ is $o(n)$: divide by $n$ and the randomness disappears, $M_n\to\mathbb{E}[X]$.
    • The CLT says the deviation is exactly of order $\sqrt{n}$: divide by $\sqrt{n}$ instead of by $n$, and the randomness does not disappear — it settles down to a fixed shape.
    So $\sqrt{n}$ is the knife's edge. Scale down harder than $\sqrt{n}$ and you get a constant (the weak law); scale down less and you get something that blows up; scale by exactly $\sqrt{n}$ and you get the normal distribution (B&T §5.4, p. 273: "what is special about this particular scaling is that it keeps the variance at a constant").

    2.1 The statement, and why standardization is forced on us

    Fix the setup for the whole section. Let $X_1,X_2,\dots$ be independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables with common mean $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and common finite variance $\sigma^2$; let

    $$S_n = X_1+X_2+\cdots+X_n$$

    be the $n$-fold sum. Here $n$ is the number of terms, $\sigma=\sqrt{\sigma^2}$ is the common standard deviation of a single $X_i$, and nothing else about the distribution of $X_i$ will ever be used.

    Why we cannot look at $S_n$ itself. Compute its two moments, using linearity of expectation and the fact that variances of independent random variables add (G2 §4):

    $$\mathbb{E}[S_n] = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_i]=n\,\mathbb{E}[X],\qquad \operatorname{var}(S_n)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{var}(X_i)=n\sigma^2,\qquad \sigma_{S_n}=\sqrt{n}\,\sigma .$$

    Both the center and the spread run away to infinity as $n$ grows, so the distribution of $S_n$ cannot converge to anything. Two fixes suggest themselves and both fail on their own:

    • Center only: $S_n-n\mathbb{E}[X]$ has mean $0$ but standard deviation $\sqrt{n}\,\sigma\to \infty$ — it spreads out without limit.
    • Divide by $n$: $M_n=S_n/n$ has mean $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and standard deviation $\sigma/\sqrt{n}\to 0$ — it collapses to a point, which is exactly the weak law of §1 and carries no shape information at all.

    The unique scaling that fixes both moments simultaneously is to subtract the mean and divide by the standard deviation.

    Definition (standardized sum; L20 slide 1). The standardized sum is $$Z_n \;=\; \frac{S_n-\mathbb{E}[S_n]}{\sigma_{S_n}} \;=\; \frac{S_n-n\,\mathbb{E}[X]}{\sqrt{n}\,\sigma} \;=\;\frac{X_1+\cdots+X_n-n\,\mathbb{E}[X]}{\sigma\sqrt{n}} .$$ The numerator recenters ($\mathbb{E}$ of it is $0$); the denominator rescales so that the spread is $1$, whatever $n$ is.

    Mean $0$ and variance $1$: already verified in §1. L20 slide 1 states these two facts without proof, and we do not need to redo the algebra here: $Z_n$ is the same standardized variable introduced in §1, Example 1.4, where both moments were checked — $\mathbb{E}[Z_n]=\big(\mathbb{E}[S_n]-n\mathbb{E}[X]\big)/(\sigma\sqrt n)=0$ by linearity, and $\operatorname{var}(Z_n)=\operatorname{var}(S_n)/(\sigma\sqrt n)^2=n\sigma^2/(n\sigma^2)=1$ by the rule $\operatorname{var}(aS+b)=a^2\operatorname{var}(S)$ (shifting does not change spread, scaling squares it — G2 §3). The variance step is the only place independence enters the bookkeeping: it is used once, inside $\operatorname{var}(S_n)=n\sigma^2$. Notation: §0 and §1 wrote the common mean as $\mu$; from here on we write $\mathbb{E}[X]$ for exactly the same quantity, matching L20 and B&T §5.4.

    Figure 2.1 shows the two steps happening to a concrete sum: panel (a) is the raw $S_{36}$, panel (b) subtracts $n\mathbb{E}[X]$, panel (c) divides by $\sigma\sqrt n$ — and only after both steps does a fixed limiting curve appear.

    Fig. 2.1 — The standardization pipeline on $S_{36}\sim\text{Binomial}(36,\tfrac12)$ (L20 slides 1 and 5). (a) The raw sum sits at $n\mathbb{E}[X]=18$ with $\sigma_{S_n}=\sqrt{np(1-p)}=3$. (b) Subtracting $n\mathbb{E}[X]$ moves it to the origin but leaves the spread at 3. (c) Dividing by $\sqrt{n}\,\sigma=3$ pins the spread at 1; the envelope is now the fixed curve $N(0,1)$, and the atom heights are drawn scaled by $\sigma_{S_n}$ so that they sit on that curve.
    The Central Limit Theorem (L20 slide 1; B&T §5.4, p. 273). Let $X_1,X_2,\dots$ be i.i.d. with mean $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and finite variance $\sigma^2$, and let $$Z_n=\frac{X_1+\cdots+X_n-n\,\mathbb{E}[X]}{\sigma\sqrt{n}} .$$ Let $Z$ be a standard normal random variable (mean $0$, variance $1$). Then for every real number $c$, $$\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}(Z_n\le c)\;=\;\mathbf{P}(Z\le c)\;=\;\Phi(c),$$ where $\Phi$ is the standard normal CDF, read from the normal tables (G3 §1).

    Three things deserve to be said out loud about this statement.

    1. The hypotheses are almost nothing. Independence, identical distribution, and a finite variance. The $X_i$ may be discrete, continuous, or mixed; bounded or unbounded; symmetric or wildly skewed. B&T §5.4 (p. 274): "Besides independence, and the implicit assumption that the mean and variance are finite, it places no other requirement on the distribution of the $X_i$."
    2. The conclusion involves only $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\sigma^2$. Those two numbers are the entire input; everything else about the distribution is washed out.
    3. What converges is a CDF, evaluated at a fixed $c$ — a statement about probabilities of events, not about densities. §2.2 is devoted to this point because it is the one students get wrong.

    Figure 2.2 makes point 2 visible: two bases that look nothing alike produce the same limiting shape, and the only thing that differs is how fast they get there.

    Fig. 2.2 — The lecture's own illustration, redrawn (L20 slide 3). Top row: the base is the discrete uniform PMF on $\{1,\dots,8\}$ — already symmetric, and by $n=2$ (the tent) it is close, by $n=4$ almost indistinguishable. Bottom row: the base is the geometric-like PMF $p_x\propto 2^{-x}$ on $\{1,\dots,6\}$ — violently skewed, still visibly skewed at $n=4$, essentially normal at $n=32$. In both rows the PMF heights are scaled by $\sigma_{S_n}$ so the panels can be compared to the same fixed $N(0,1)$ curve. Deviation from the slide: L20 slide 3 draws its symmetric group at $n=1,2,4,32$ but its skewed group at $n=1,8,16,32$; we redraw both rows at the same $n=1,2,4,32$ so that the two bases can be read column by column — which is the whole point being made. Symmetry buys you a great deal of speed; skewness costs you.
    Interpretation (why "universal" is the right word — L20 slide 2). The slide lists three payoffs, and each is worth unpacking.
    • Universality: only means and variances matter. Suppose two engineers model the same noisy measurement, one with a uniform error distribution, one with a triangular one. If they match the mean and the variance, then for a sum of many such errors their answers agree, no matter how different their models look. This is why arguing about the exact shape of a small random effect is usually a waste of time: after summing, only two numbers survive.
    • An accurate computational shortcut. Computing $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le c)$ exactly means an $n$-fold convolution — a sum over an exponentially growing set of ways to reach $c$. The CLT replaces it with one subtraction, one division and one table lookup. In Example 2.1 below the exact answer is a sum of 22 binomial coefficients; the CLT answer is $\Phi(1.1667)$.
    • Justification of normal models. Whenever a physical quantity is the accumulation of many small independent contributions — thermal noise, measurement error, the height of a person as a sum of many genetic and environmental increments — the CLT predicts it should look normal. That prediction is confirmed empirically over and over, and the CLT explains why rather than just noting the fact (B&T §5.4, p. 274).
    Gotcha (finite variance is not decoration). Drop the finite-variance hypothesis and the theorem is false, not merely unproven. If the $X_i$ are Cauchy — density $f(x)=\frac{1}{\pi(1+x^2)}$, for which $\mathbb{E}[|X|]=\infty$ — then $S_n/n$ has exactly the same Cauchy distribution as a single $X_1$, for every $n$. No amount of averaging helps, and no normal limit appears. Whenever you invoke the CLT, you are quietly asserting $\sigma^2<\infty$.
    Practice 2.1. $X_1,\dots,X_n$ are i.i.d. with $\mathbb{E}[X]=4$ and $\sigma^2=25$. Write down $\mathbb{E}[S_{100}]$, $\operatorname{var}(S_{100})$, $\sigma_{S_{100}}$, and the standardized variable $Z_{100}$ explicitly.
    Answer

    $\mathbb{E}[S_{100}]=100\times 4=400$ by linearity. $\operatorname{var}(S_{100})=100\times 25=2500$ because independent variances add. $\sigma_{S_{100}}=\sqrt{2500}=50$ — note it is not $100\times 5$; standard deviations do not add, variances do. Hence $Z_{100}=(S_{100}-400)/50$.

    Practice 2.2. True or false: if the $X_i$ are already normal, the CLT is needed to conclude that $S_n$ is normal.
    Answer

    False, and the distinction matters. A sum of independent normal random variables is exactly normal for every $n$, including $n=1$ — that is a property of the normal family (proved by convolution in G3 §4), not a limit theorem. Consequently $Z_n$ is exactly $N(0,1)$ for all $n$ and the max CDF gap is $0$. The CLT is only interesting when the base distribution is not normal.

    2.2 What exactly does the theorem say? (and what it does not)

    L20 slide 2 puts the warning in two lines: "CDF of $Z_n$ converges to normal CDF — not a statement about convergence of PDFs or PMFs." Here is why that distinction is not pedantry.

    Gotcha (the single most common CLT error). The CLT says $$\mathbf{P}(Z_n\le c)\to\Phi(c)\quad\text{for each fixed } c.$$ It does not say $p_{Z_n}(c)\to\varphi(c)$, and for discrete $X_i$ that latter statement is flatly false. Take $X_i$ Bernoulli$(1/2)$, $n=16$. Then $S_{16}$ is an integer, so $Z_{16}$ takes only the 17 values $(k-8)/2$, $k=0,\dots,16$, and $\mathbf{P}(Z_{16}=z)=0$ for every other $z$ — including $z=0.1$, where the normal density is $\varphi(0.1)=0.397$. Worse, at the values it does hit, the probabilities go to zero: the largest atom of $Z_{16}$ has probability $0.196381$, of $Z_{64}$ only $0.099347$, and it keeps shrinking like $1/\sqrt{n}$. A PMF whose every value tends to $0$ cannot converge to a density. What is happening is that the atoms get closer together (spacing $1/(\sigma\sqrt n)$) at exactly the rate their heights shrink, so their accumulated mass — the CDF — converges perfectly well. Figure 2.3 puts the two views side by side.
    Fig. 2.3 — Why the CLT is a CDF statement (L20 slide 2). (a) The standardized PMF of $Z_n$ for $X_i\sim$ Bernoulli$(1/2)$: as $n$ goes from 16 (blue) to 64 (green) every individual probability halves in size and doubles in count; nothing converges to the orange density. (b) The same $n=16$ distribution as a CDF: the staircase already hugs $\Phi$, with a worst-case vertical gap of $0.0982$ over all $z$ (key widget_parity, row $n=16$). The gap shrinks like $1/\sqrt n$: $0.0700$ at $n=32$, $0.0497$ at $n=64$.
    Interpretation (what the theorem buys you in practice). Since a CDF determines all interval probabilities, CDF convergence is enough for everything you actually want: $$\mathbf{P}(a\le Z_n\le b)=\mathbf{P}(Z_n\le b)-\mathbf{P}(Z_n<a)\;\longrightarrow\;\Phi(b)-\Phi(a).$$ The only quantity CDF convergence does not hand you is the probability of a single point — and §2.4 shows how the $1/2$ correction recovers even that, by turning "the atom at $k$" into "the interval $[k-\tfrac12,\,k+\tfrac12]$", which is an interval probability.

    Normal approximation: treating $S_n$ as if it were normal

    L20 slide 2 makes the practical move explicit: treat $Z_n$ as if it were normal, and therefore treat $S_n$ as if it were normal too. The second half follows from the first because normality is preserved by linear transformations (G3 §1): if $Z_n\approx N(0,1)$ and $S_n=n\mathbb{E}[X]+\sigma\sqrt{n}\,Z_n$, then $S_n$ is approximately normal with mean $n\mathbb{E}[X]$ and variance $n\sigma^2$. Both descriptions give identical numbers; use whichever is less error-prone for the problem at hand.

    Gotcha (moderate $n$ — L20 slide 2). The slide asks "Can we use it when $n$ is moderate?" and answers: "Yes, but no nice theorems to this effect. Symmetry helps a lot." Take that literally. There is no universal threshold — no "$n\ge 30$" rule that is actually a theorem. What governs the accuracy is how far the base distribution is from normal, and skewness is the dominant defect. The max CDF gap $\sup_c|\mathbf{P}(Z_n\le c)-\Phi(c)|$ computed exactly in computes/g6_s2.py (key gap_table) makes the point:
    base distribution$n=1$$n=4$$n=16$$n=32$
    uniform on $[0,1]$ (symmetric)$0.05721$$0.00738$$0.00174$$0.00087$
    Exponential$(1)$ (skewed)$0.15865$$0.06670$$0.03328$$0.02352$
    Bernoulli$(0.5)$ (symmetric, lattice)$0.34134$$0.18750$$0.09819$$0.06997$
    Bernoulli$(0.1)$ (skewed, lattice)$0.53056$$0.40361$$0.20619$$0.14721$
    At $n=32$ the symmetric continuous case is accurate to $0.001$ while the skewed lattice case is still off by $0.15$ — a factor of $170$. B&T §5.4 (p. 277) says the same thing in words: "if the $X_i$ are uniform, then $S_8$ is already very close to normal. But if the $X_i$ are, say, exponential, a significantly larger $n$ will be needed."
    Intuition (the $1/\sqrt n$ rate, and where it comes from). The bottom three rows of that table shrink by roughly $\sqrt{2}$ when $n$ doubles — from $n=16$ to $n=32$, $0.03328\to 0.02352$, $0.09819\to 0.06997$, $0.20619\to 0.14721$, each a factor of about $1.41$. That is the signature of a $1/\sqrt n$ rate. The uniform row is the exception: it runs $0.00174\to 0.00087$, a factor of $2.01$, i.e. a $1/n$ rate. The reason is that the leading correction term in the Edgeworth expansion is proportional to the skewness of the base, so for a base that is both symmetric and non-lattice — like the uniform on $[0,1]$ — that term vanishes identically and the $1/\sqrt n$ bound below, which only ever sees the third absolute moment, is simply not tight. Where the rate really is $1/\sqrt n$ it is a theorem: the Berry–Esseen bound says $$\sup_c\big|\mathbf{P}(Z_n\le c)-\Phi(c)\big|\;\le\;\frac{C\,\rho}{\sigma^3\sqrt n},\qquad \rho=\mathbb{E}\big[|X-\mathbb{E}[X]|^3\big],$$ with an absolute constant $C\approx 0.4748$. For Bernoulli$(0.5)$, $\rho=\tfrac12(\tfrac12)^3+ \tfrac12(\tfrac12)^3=\tfrac18$ and $\sigma^3=(1/2)^3=1/8$, so the bound is $0.4748/\sqrt n$: at $n=16$ that is $0.11870$ and at $n=32$ it is $0.08393$ (keys berry_esseen_bern5) — correctly bounding the true gaps $0.09819$ and $0.06997$, and not by much. Notice what the bound depends on: the third absolute central moment, i.e. exactly the asymmetry of the base distribution. This is the theorem behind "symmetry helps a lot".

    Widget: watch the theorem happen

    Everything above is a claim about a limit, which is hard to trust from a static picture. The widget below computes the exact distribution of the standardized sum $Z_n$ — no simulation, no sampling noise — for four very different base distributions, and reports the one number that the theorem actually controls: the largest gap between the CDF of $Z_n$ and $\Phi$. Drag $n$, or press animate and watch. Switch the checkbox to see the same thing as a CDF, where the convergence is visible; the density view shows a shape converging while its individual atoms vanish.

    The four bases (all cross-checked against computes/g6_s2.py, key widget_parity): the discrete uniform on $\{1,\dots,8\}$ used on L20 slide 3; the exponential with rate 1 (the standard "how bad can skew be?" example, handled exactly through the Erlang/Gamma CDF); Bernoulli$(p)$ with $p$ adjustable, which is the binomial case of §2.4; and a bimodal base putting mass $1/4$ on each of $\{0,1,5,6\}$, which looks nothing like a bell and converges anyway.

    Practice 2.3. In the widget, set the base to Bernoulli and compare $p=0.5$ with $p=0.1$ at $n=16$. Why is one so much worse than the other, and which quantity in the Berry–Esseen bound explains it?
    Answer

    At $n=16$ the gaps are about $0.098$ for $p=0.5$ and $0.206$ for $p=0.1$ (key gap_table, row $n=16$, columns bern5 and bern1) — the skewed coin is roughly twice as bad. The Berry–Esseen ratio $\rho/\sigma^3$ is the culprit. For Bernoulli$(p)$ with $q=1-p$, $$\rho=\mathbb{E}|X-p|^3 = p\,q^3+q\,p^3 = pq(p^2+q^2),\qquad \sigma^3=(pq)^{3/2},$$ so $\rho/\sigma^3=(p^2+q^2)/\sqrt{pq}$. At $p=0.5$ this is $0.5/0.5=1$; at $p=0.1$ it is $0.82/0.3=2.73$ — nearly three times larger, and the observed gap ratio tracks it. Concretely: with $p=0.1$ and $n=16$ the sum is $0$ with probability $0.9^{16}=0.185$, a single atom carrying $18.5\%$ of the mass, which no smooth curve can imitate. Small $p$ needs large $n$; B&T §5.4 p. 279 puts the working threshold at $n\approx 40$–$50$ when $p\approx 1/2$, and "a larger value of $n$" when $p$ is near $0$ or $1$.

    Practice 2.4. Set the base to bimodal on $\{0,1,5,6\}$ — a distribution with a hole in the middle, whose density at its own mean is zero. Predict what the standardized sum looks like at $n=2$ and at $n=16$, then check.
    Answer

    At $n=2$ the sum takes values $0,\dots,12$ and still has visible lumps (the gap is $0.14076$); by $n=16$ the lumps are gone and the gap is $0.02221$; at $n=64$ it is $0.00986$ (key widget_parity). The base is symmetric about its mean $3$, which is why it converges faster than Bernoulli$(0.5)$ despite looking far stranger: shape irregularity is smoothed by convolution almost immediately, whereas skew is not. This is the CLT's universality made uncomfortable — you cannot tell from the $n=16$ picture that the base had a hole in it.

    2.3 Using it: the normal approximation recipe

    All CLT computations follow the same four steps, and B&T §5.4 (p. 275) boxes them as a procedure. Here it is with the two steps the book leaves implicit made explicit.

    Recipe (normal approximation for $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le c)$; B&T §5.4 p. 275).
    1. Identify the summands. Write the event in terms of a sum $S_n=X_1+\cdots+X_n$ of i.i.d. variables. Sometimes this needs a reformulation — see Example 2.3, where "how many parts in 320 minutes" becomes "how long do 100 parts take".
    2. Get $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\sigma^2$ for a single term, then $$\mathbb{E}[S_n]=n\,\mathbb{E}[X],\qquad \operatorname{var}(S_n)=n\sigma^2,\qquad \sigma_{S_n}=\sigma\sqrt{n}.$$
    3. Standardize the threshold. Compute the normalized value $$z=\frac{c-n\,\mathbb{E}[X]}{\sigma\sqrt{n}} .$$ This is one number; it says "the threshold $c$ sits $z$ standard deviations above the mean of $S_n$".
    4. Look up $\Phi(z)$ and read off $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le c)\approx\Phi(z)$. For the other tail use $\mathbf{P}(S_n>c)\approx 1-\Phi(z)$; for negative arguments use the symmetry $\Phi(-z)=1-\Phi(z)$, since normal tables list only $z\ge 0$; for an interval use $\mathbf{P}(a\le S_n\le c)\approx \Phi(z_c)-\Phi(z_a)$.
    Two extra steps you must not skip: (5) if $S_n$ is integer-valued, apply the $1/2$ correction of §2.4 before looking anything up; (6) if $\sigma^2$ is unknown but bounded, use the largest possible $\sigma^2$ — this makes the tail estimate an upper bound rather than an estimate (§2.5). Figure 2.5 at the end of §2.5 turns this recipe, together with §1's bounds, into a single decision chart: read it top to bottom to decide which tool to reach for before running these steps.
    Example 2.1 (L20 slide 5 — [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE]: the binomial the slide leaves blank; = B&T Example 5.12). Let $S_n$ be Binomial$(n,p)$ with $n=36$, $p=0.5$. Find $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le 21)$. The slide poses the question, leaves a blank where the lecturer works it on the board, and prints only the exact value $\sum_{k=0}^{21}\binom{36}{k}(1/2)^{36}=0.8785$.
    Solution (all six steps, plus the exact value recomputed)

    Step 1 — the summands. A Binomial$(n,p)$ variable is a sum: $S_n=X_1+\cdots+X_{36}$ with $X_i$ i.i.d. Bernoulli$(0.5)$, $X_i=1$ if the $i$th trial succeeds (L20 slide 5). So the CLT applies with no reformulation.

    Step 2 — moments. For a Bernoulli$(p)$ variable, $\mathbb{E}[X]=p=0.5$ and $\sigma^2=p(1-p)=0.5\times0.5=0.25$ (G2 §3). Hence $$\mathbb{E}[S_{36}]=np=36\times0.5=18,\qquad \operatorname{var}(S_{36})=np(1-p)=36\times0.25=9,\qquad \sigma_{S_{36}}=\sqrt{9}=3 .$$ [DERIVATION GAP filled: the slide uses $18$ and $3$ on the next two slides without ever computing them.]

    Step 3 — standardize. With $c=21$, $$z=\frac{21-18}{3}=\frac{3}{3}=1.0 .$$

    Step 4 — look up. $\Phi(1.00)=0.8413$ from the normal table ($0.841345$ to six places), so $$\mathbf{P}(S_{36}\le 21)\approx 0.841345 .$$

    Step 5 — the correction (previewing §2.4). $S_{36}$ is integer-valued, so replace the cut $21$ by $21.5$: $$z=\frac{21.5-18}{3}=\frac{3.5}{3}=1.166667,\qquad \Phi(1.1667)=0.878327 .$$ (The table value at the rounded $z=1.17$ is $0.878999$; B&T rounds this to $0.879$.)

    Step 6 — no unknown variance here, so nothing to do.

    Compare with the exact answer. Recomputing the slide's sum in computes/g6_s2.py (key b36_exact_le21): $$\sum_{k=0}^{21}\binom{36}{k}\Big(\frac12\Big)^{36}=0.878508,$$ confirming the slide's $0.8785$. The errors are $$|0.841345-0.878508|=0.037163\quad\text{(no correction)},\qquad |0.878327-0.878508|=0.000180\quad\text{(corrected)} .$$ The $1/2$ correction improves the answer by a factor of $206$. That is the whole point of §2.4.

    Example 2.2 (B&T Example 5.9 — the airplane cargo). We load 100 packages whose weights are independent and uniformly distributed between 5 and 50 pounds. What is the probability that the total weight exceeds 3000 pounds?
    Solution

    Step 1. $S_{100}=X_1+\cdots+X_{100}$, $X_i\sim$ Uniform$[5,50]$ i.i.d., $X_i$ = weight of the $i$th package in pounds. We want $\mathbf{P}(S_{100}>3000)$.

    Step 2. For a uniform on $[a,b]$ (G3 §1), $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\frac{a+b}{2}=\frac{5+50}{2}=27.5,\qquad \sigma^2=\frac{(b-a)^2}{12}=\frac{45^2}{12}=\frac{2025}{12}=168.75 .$$ Hence $\mathbb{E}[S_{100}]=100\times 27.5=2750$, $\operatorname{var}(S_{100})=100\times 168.75=16875$, and $\sigma_{S_{100}}=\sqrt{16875}=129.903811$.

    Step 3. $\displaystyle z=\frac{3000-2750}{129.903811}=\frac{250}{129.903811}=1.924501$. (The book rounds the denominator to $129.9$ and reports $z=1.92$.)

    Step 4. $\Phi(1.924501)=0.972854$, so $$\mathbf{P}(S_{100}\le 3000)\approx 0.972854 \;\Longrightarrow\; \mathbf{P}(S_{100}>3000)\approx 1-0.972854=\boxed{0.027146}.$$ Using the book's rounded $z=1.92$ gives $\Phi(1.92)=0.972571$ and the answer $0.027429$, which the book prints as $0.0274$. The two differ only through the rounding of $z$ — a reminder that quoting four decimals from a two-decimal table lookup is false precision.

    Step 5. The weights are continuous, so there is no $1/2$ correction. Applying one here would be an error (see the Gotcha in §2.4).

    Example 2.3 (B&T Example 5.10 — the machine, where step 1 does the work). A machine processes parts one at a time; processing times are independent and uniform on $[1,5]$. Let $N_{320}$ be the number of parts finished within 320 time units. Approximate $\mathbf{P}(N_{320}\ge 100)$.
    Solution

    Step 1 — the reformulation. $N_{320}$ is not a sum of i.i.d. variables, so the CLT does not apply to it directly. But the two events $$\{N_{320}\ge 100\}\quad\text{and}\quad \{S_{100}\le 320\},\qquad S_{100}=X_1+\cdots+X_{100},$$ are the same event: at least 100 parts are done by time 320 exactly when the total time for the first 100 parts is at most 320. (This "inverse" trick — counting process versus arrival times — is the same duality used for Poisson processes in G4 §3.) Now $S_{100}$ is an i.i.d. sum.

    Step 2. $\mathbb{E}[X]=(1+5)/2=3$ and $\sigma^2=(5-1)^2/12=16/12=1.333333$. So $\mathbb{E}[S_{100}]=300$, $\operatorname{var}(S_{100})=133.333333$, $\sigma_{S_{100}}=11.547005$.

    Step 3. $\displaystyle z=\frac{320-300}{11.547005}=\frac{20}{11.547005}=1.732051$ (the book writes $1.73$).

    Step 4. $\Phi(1.732051)=0.958368$, so $\mathbf{P}(N_{320}\ge 100)\approx\boxed{0.958368}$; with the book's $z=1.73$, $\Phi(1.73)=0.958185$, printed as $0.9582$.

    How good is it? The exact answer is available here, because a sum of 100 uniforms on $[1,5]$ is $100+4\times(\text{Irwin–Hall}_{100})$: $\mathbf{P}(S_{100}\le 320)=0.958368$ (key e510_exact). The CLT error is below $10^{-6}$ — with a symmetric continuous base and $n=100$, the approximation is essentially exact.

    Example 2.4 (rec21 P2 = B&T Problem 5.10, p. 290 — the gadget factory). The solution sheet for this problem says, in full, "2. Check online solutions." [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE: parts (a), (b), (c) are all unsolved in both rec21 PDFs.] Here it is, complete. A factory produces $X_n$ gadgets on day $n$; the $X_n$ are i.i.d. with mean 5 and variance 9.
    (a) Approximate the probability that the total produced in 100 days is less than 440.
    (b) Find approximately the largest $n$ with $\mathbf{P}(X_1+\cdots+X_n\ge 200+5n)\le 0.05$.
    (c) Let $N$ be the first day on which the cumulative total exceeds 1000. Approximate $\mathbf{P}(N\ge 220)$.
    Solution (all three parts, from scratch)

    Throughout: $\mathbb{E}[X]=5$, $\sigma^2=9$, so $\sigma=3$. Note that the distribution of $X_n$ is never specified — which is precisely the point of the CLT, and precisely why the problem is solvable.

    (a) Here $n=100$ and the threshold is $c=440$.

    Moments of the sum: $\mathbb{E}[S_{100}]=100\times5=500$ and $\operatorname{var}(S_{100})=100\times9=900$, so $\sigma_{S_{100}}=\sqrt{900}=30$.

    Standardize: $\displaystyle z=\frac{440-500}{30}=\frac{-60}{30}=-2$.

    Look up, using $\Phi(-2)=1-\Phi(2)$: $$\mathbf{P}(S_{100}<440)\approx\Phi(-2)=1-\Phi(2)=1-0.977250=\boxed{0.022750},$$ about $2.3\%$. (With a two-decimal table, $1-0.9772=0.0228$.) No $1/2$ correction is applied because the problem never says the $X_n$ are integers; if they are — gadgets usually being countable — then $\mathbf{P}(S_{100}<440)=\mathbf{P}(S_{100}\le 439)$ and the corrected cut is $439.5$, giving $z=-2.016667$ and $0.021865$ (key g_a_prob_corr). The two answers differ by $0.0009$; we report the uncorrected one, as the problem gives no lattice information.

    (b) The event is $S_n\ge 200+5n$, i.e. the sum exceeds its own mean $5n$ by 200. Subtract the mean from both sides and divide by $\sigma\sqrt n=3\sqrt n$: $$S_n\ge 200+5n \iff S_n-5n\ge 200 \iff \frac{S_n-5n}{3\sqrt n}\ge \frac{200}{3\sqrt n} \iff Z_n\ge \frac{200}{3\sqrt n} .$$ Note that the threshold on the right grows as $n$ shrinks: with few days it is very hard to run 200 gadgets ahead of schedule, and easy with many. So the constraint will cap $n$ from above, as the problem's wording ("largest value of $n$") anticipates.

    Apply the CLT and the requirement: $$\mathbf{P}(S_n\ge 200+5n)\approx 1-\Phi\!\Big(\frac{200}{3\sqrt n}\Big)\le 0.05 \iff \Phi\!\Big(\frac{200}{3\sqrt n}\Big)\ge 0.95 .$$ From the normal table, $\Phi(1.645)=0.950015$, so the requirement is $$\frac{200}{3\sqrt n}\ge 1.644854 \iff \sqrt n \le \frac{200}{3\times 1.644854}=\frac{200}{4.934561}=40.530455 \iff n\le 40.530455^2=1642.717820 .$$ Since $n$ is an integer, the largest admissible value is $$\boxed{n=1642}.$$ Check both sides (keys g_b_check_1642, g_b_check_1643): at $n=1642$ the probability is $1-\Phi(1.645213)=0.049963\le0.05$ ✓; at $n=1643$ it is $1-\Phi(1.644712)=0.050015 >0.05$ ✗. Using the rounded table value $1.645$ instead of $1.644854$ gives $n\le 1642.425$ and the same answer $1642$ — the rounding is harmless here.

    (c) The trick is the same duality as in Example 2.3, applied carefully. $N$ is the first day on which the running total exceeds 1000. Then $$\{N\ge 220\}=\{\text{the total after } 219 \text{ days has not yet exceeded } 1000\}=\{S_{219}\le 1000\}.$$ (Reason: $N\ge220$ means $N$ is not $1,2,\dots,219$, i.e. the total never exceeded 1000 during the first 219 days; since the total is nondecreasing, "never exceeded by day 219" is the same as "the day-219 total is at most 1000".) Now $S_{219}$ is an i.i.d. sum and the CLT applies.

    Moments: $\mathbb{E}[S_{219}]=219\times5=1095$, $\operatorname{var}(S_{219})=219\times9=1971$, $\sigma_{S_{219}}=\sqrt{1971}=3\sqrt{219}=44.395946$.

    Standardize: $\displaystyle z=\frac{1000-1095}{44.395946}=\frac{-95}{44.395946}=-2.139835$.

    Look up: $\Phi(-2.139835)=1-\Phi(2.139835)=1-0.983816=\boxed{0.016184}$, about $1.6\%$. (With a table rounded to $z=-2.14$: $0.0162$.)

    Gap worth flagging. A frequent slip here is to use $S_{220}$ instead of $S_{219}$, which gives $z=-2.247333$ and $0.012309$ (key g_c_alt_prob) — a $24\%$ relative error, caused purely by an off-by-one in the event translation. Always translate the event first, then count days.

    Practice 2.5. The daily number of e-mails you receive has mean 40 and standard deviation 12, independently across days. Approximate the probability that over 30 days you receive more than 1300.
    Answer

    $\mathbb{E}[S_{30}]=30\times40=1200$; $\operatorname{var}(S_{30})=30\times12^2=30\times144=4320$; $\sigma_{S_{30}}=\sqrt{4320}=65.727$. Then $z=(1300-1200)/65.727=1.5215$, and $\mathbf{P}(S_{30}>1300)\approx 1-\Phi(1.5215)=1-0.9360=0.0640$. (With the $1/2$ correction for an integer count, cut at $1300.5$: $z=1.5291$, answer $0.0631$.) Note we were given the standard deviation, not the variance — squaring it before multiplying by $n$ is the step that gets forgotten.

    Practice 2.6. A machine's service times are i.i.d. exponential with rate $\lambda=1/4$ per minute. Approximate $\mathbf{P}(\text{60 services take at most 250 minutes})$, and compare with the exact Erlang answer.
    Answer

    For Exponential$(\lambda)$, $\mathbb{E}[X]=1/\lambda=4$ and $\sigma^2=1/\lambda^2=16$ (G3 §1). So $\mathbb{E}[S_{60}]=240$, $\operatorname{var}(S_{60})=960$, $\sigma_{S_{60}}=30.983867$, and $z=(250-240)/30.983867=0.322749$, giving $\Phi(0.322749)=0.626557$. The exact value (an Erlang / Gamma$(60,1/4)$ CDF) is $0.641053$ (keys pr_exp_ans, pr_exp_exact) — an error of $0.0145$, which is large for $n=60$. The exponential is strongly skewed (skewness $2$), and the residual skewness of the sum is $2/\sqrt{60}=0.258$; this is the "$n$ moderate, no nice theorems" warning made numerical.

    2.4 De Moivre–Laplace: the $1/2$ correction for integer-valued sums

    Everything so far approximated a discrete distribution by a continuous one without comment. For $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le c)$ with $c$ far from the mean that is tolerable; near the mean it is not, and for $\mathbf{P}(S_n=k)$ it is a disaster — the normal model assigns probability $0$ to every single point. The fix is a half-unit shift, and it is worth deriving rather than memorizing.

    The derivation, on the lecture's own example

    Take $S_n\sim$ Binomial$(36,0.5)$ again, with $\mathbb{E}[S_n]=18$ and $\sigma_{S_n}=3$ (Example 2.1). The starting observation is L20 slide 6's first line:

    $$\mathbf{P}(S_n\le 21)=\mathbf{P}(S_n<22),\qquad\text{because } S_n \text{ is an integer.}$$

    Both statements are exactly true for the discrete variable: there is no probability mass strictly between 21 and 22. But the normal approximation does not know that. Approximating the left-hand side puts the cut at 21 and gives $\Phi\big(\frac{21-18}{3}\big)=\Phi(1)=0.841345$; approximating the right-hand side puts the cut at 22 and gives $\Phi\big(\frac{22-18}{3}\big)=\Phi(1.333333)=0.908789$. Two answers, differing by $0.067$, for the same exact quantity. The approximation is ambiguous, and the ambiguity is exactly the mass of the bar at 21.

    Recipe (the compromise — L20 slide 6). Split the difference: cut halfway between the two integers, at $21.5$. In general, when $S_n$ is integer-valued, $$\mathbf{P}(S_n\le k)\approx \Phi\!\left(\frac{k+\tfrac12-n\mathbb{E}[X]}{\sigma\sqrt n}\right), \qquad \mathbf{P}(S_n\ge k)\approx 1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{k-\tfrac12-n\mathbb{E}[X]}{\sigma\sqrt n}\right).$$ The rule behind both: the probability bar standing at the integer $k$ is matched by the area under the normal density over the interval $[k-\tfrac12,\,k+\tfrac12]$. Every event of the form "$S_n$ lies in a set of integers" is then approximated by the area over the union of those unit intervals. [DERIVATION GAP filled: L20 slide 6 draws this picture but never states the rule in words nor evaluates it.] Figure 2.4 draws both halves of this argument — the bar-to-area match in panel (a), and the sliver the uncorrected cut throws away in panel (b).
    Fig. 2.4 — The $1/2$ correction, redrawing the hand sketch of L20 slide 6 (B&T §5.4, its Figure 5.3). (a) The bar of the Binomial$(36,\tfrac12)$ PMF at $k=19$ has exact height $0.125110$; the shaded area of the normal density over $[18.5,19.5]$ is $0.125279$ — agreement to three decimals. (b) Cutting the CDF at $21$ omits the right half of the bar at $21$: the missing sliver is worth $0.878327-0.841345=0.036982$, which is nearly the whole error of the uncorrected approximation ($0.037163$).

    Why "half a bar" is the right amount, quantitatively. The uncorrected cut at $k$ captures the normal area up to $k$; the bar at $k$ occupies $[k-\frac12,k+\frac12]$, so the cut at $k$ picks up its left half and throws away its right half. The discarded mass is approximately half the bar, $\tfrac12 p_{S_n}(k)$. For $k=21$ the exact bar height is $p_{S_{36}}(21)=0.081024$, so the discarded mass is $\tfrac12\times 0.081024=0.040512$ — within $9\%$ of the observed error $0.037163$, the small remainder being the curvature of the density across the bar. The correction adds that missing half back, and the residual error collapses from $3.7\times10^{-2}$ to $1.8\times10^{-4}$.

    De Moivre–Laplace approximation to the binomial (L20 slide 7; B&T §5.4, p. 279). If $S_n$ is Binomial$(n,p)$, $n$ is large, and $k\le l$ are nonnegative integers, then $$\mathbf{P}(k\le S_n\le l)\;\approx\; \Phi\!\left(\frac{l+\tfrac12-np}{\sqrt{np(1-p)}}\right)-\Phi\!\left(\frac{k-\tfrac12-np}{\sqrt{np(1-p)}}\right).$$ Taking $k=l$ gives an approximation to the PMF itself — something the plain CLT could never provide. B&T adds the practical rider: when $p\approx1/2$ this is very good for $n$ as low as 40 or 50; when $p$ is near 0 or 1 a larger $n$ is needed.
    Example 2.5 (L20 slide 7 — the PMF of the binomial, every step). With $n=36$, $p=0.5$, approximate $\mathbf{P}(S_n=19)$ and compare with the exact $\binom{36}{19}(1/2)^{36}$.
    Solution (the slide's chain, with the two gaps filled)

    Step 1 — turn the point into an interval. Because $S_n$ takes only integer values, $$\mathbf{P}(S_n=19)=\mathbf{P}(18.5\le S_n\le 19.5)$$ exactly — the interval $[18.5,19.5]$ contains no integer other than 19. [Gap filled: the slide asserts this equality without saying that it depends on integrality.] This is the whole trick: a point event has been rewritten as an interval event, which is what a CDF limit can handle.

    Step 2 — standardize both endpoints. Recall $\mathbb{E}[S_n]=18$ and $\sigma_{S_n}=3$ (carried over from Example 2.1 — the slide reuses them silently). Subtracting 18 and dividing by 3 preserves the inequalities: $$18.5\le S_n\le 19.5 \iff \frac{18.5-18}{3}\le \frac{S_n-18}{3}\le \frac{19.5-18}{3} \iff 0.166667\le Z_n\le 0.5 .$$ (The slide rounds $0.5/3=0.1\overline{6}$ to $0.17$.)

    Step 3 — apply the CLT to the interval. $$\mathbf{P}(S_n=19)\approx \mathbf{P}(0.166667\le Z\le 0.5) =\mathbf{P}(Z\le 0.5)-\mathbf{P}(Z\le 0.166667) =\Phi(0.5)-\Phi(0.166667).$$ The middle step is just $\mathbf{P}(a\le Z\le b)=F(b)-F(a)$ for a continuous variable (G3 §1), where the two boundary points contribute nothing.

    Step 4 — the table. $\Phi(0.5)=0.691462$ and $\Phi(0.166667)=0.566184$, so $$\mathbf{P}(S_n=19)\approx 0.691462-0.566184=\boxed{0.125279}.$$ With the slide's rounded arguments: $\Phi(0.5)-\Phi(0.17)=0.6915-0.5675=0.1240$, which is what the slide prints as $0.124$.

    Step 5 — the exact value. $\binom{36}{19}=8\,597\,496\,600$ and $$\binom{36}{19}\Big(\frac12\Big)^{36}=0.125110$$ (key b36_exact_eq19), matching the slide's $0.1251$. The de Moivre–Laplace error is $0.000169$, a relative error of $0.135\%$. Rounding $z$ to two decimals, as the slide does, costs more ($0.124$ versus the true $0.1251$, a relative error of $0.9\%$) than the approximation itself — the table lookup, not the CLT, is the accuracy bottleneck here.

    The comparison table the lecture never prints

    Exact versus plain-CLT versus $1/2$-corrected, all for Binomial$(36,0.5)$, computed in computes/g6_s2.py (keys b36_table, b36_pmf_table):

    eventexactplain CLTerrorwith $1/2$ correctionerror
    $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le 18)$$0.566030$$0.500000$$-0.066030$$0.566184$$+0.000154$
    $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le 21)$$0.878508$$0.841345$$-0.037163$$0.878327$$-0.000180$
    $\mathbf{P}(S_n=19)$$0.125110$$0.000000$$-0.125110$$0.125279$$+0.000169$
    $\mathbf{P}(16\le S_n\le 20)$$0.594968$$0.495015$$-0.099953$$0.595343$$+0.000375$
    $\mathbf{P}(S_n\ge 24)$$0.032623$$0.022750$$-0.009873$$0.033377$$+0.000754$

    Read the two error columns side by side: the corrected column is smaller by a factor of between $13$ (the far tail) and $\infty$ (the point event, where the plain CLT returns literally zero). Over all integers $k$, the worst CDF error is $0.066030$ without the correction (attained at $k=18$, the mean) and $0.000754$ with it — a factor of $87.6$ (keys b36_maxgap_plain, b36_maxgap_corr). Notice also that the plain-CLT error is always negative in the lower tail: the uncorrected cut systematically discards half a bar, so it systematically underestimates. A correction that removes a systematic bias is worth far more than its arithmetic cost.

    Gotcha (three ways to misuse the correction).
    • Do not apply it to continuous variables. In Example 2.2 the package weights are uniform on $[5,50]$; there are no "bars" and no gaps between values, so shifting the cut by $0.5$ simply introduces an error of $0.5/\sigma_{S_n}$ in $z$. The correction exists to repair a discretization artifact; with no discretization there is nothing to repair.
    • Get the direction right. For an upper tail you must widen, not shift blindly: $\mathbf{P}(S_n\ge k)$ uses $k-\tfrac12$, while $\mathbf{P}(S_n\le k)$ uses $k+\tfrac12$. The mnemonic is "always include the whole bar at $k$", so the cut moves away from the region you are keeping. Getting it backwards makes the answer worse than no correction at all.
    • Watch strict versus weak inequalities. For integers, $\mathbf{P}(S_n<k)= \mathbf{P}(S_n\le k-1)$, whose corrected cut is $k-\tfrac12$ — not $k+\tfrac12$. Rewrite every strict inequality as a weak one before correcting.
    Gotcha (the correction does not rescue a bad $p$). The $1/2$ correction repairs the lattice error; it cannot repair skew. For Binomial$(50,0.1)$ (key b50_table), where $np=5$ and $\sigma=2.121320$:
    eventexactplainerrorcorrectederror
    $\mathbf{P}(S\le 2)$$0.111729$$0.078650$$-0.033079$$0.119296$$+0.007568$
    $\mathbf{P}(S\le 5)$$0.616123$$0.500000$$-0.116123$$0.593168$$-0.022955$
    $\mathbf{P}(S\le 8)$$0.942133$$0.921350$$-0.020782$$0.950520$$+0.008387$
    The correction still helps — errors drop by a factor of 2 to 5 — but the residual $0.023$ is about thirty times the worst corrected residual we saw at $p=0.5$ ($0.000754$, key b36_maxgap_corr). When $np$ is small, the right tool is the Poisson approximation, not the normal one; that is the content of L20 slide 8 (see §2.6).
    Practice 2.7. A fair coin is flipped 1000 times. Approximate $\mathbf{P}(\text{at least 530 heads})$, with and without the correction, and compare with the exact binomial value.
    Answer

    $\mathbb{E}[S]=500$, $\operatorname{var}(S)=1000\times0.25=250$, $\sigma_S=15.811388$. Upper tail, so the corrected cut is $529.5$: $$z=\frac{529.5-500}{15.811388}=1.865744,\qquad \mathbf{P}(S\ge 530)\approx 1-\Phi(1.865744)=0.031039 .$$ Uncorrected ($z=30/15.811388=1.897367$): $0.028890$. Exact: $1-F_{\text{Bin}}(529)=0.031012$ (keys pr_coin_ans, pr_coin_plain, pr_coin_exact). The corrected answer is off by $0.000027$, the uncorrected one by $0.002122$ — a factor of 78. For contrast, Chebyshev applied to the same question (using symmetry to halve the two-sided bound) gives only $\le 0.138889$.

    Practice 2.8. Three hundred fair dice are rolled. Approximate $\mathbf{P}(1000\le \text{total} \le 1100)$.
    Answer

    One die: $\mathbb{E}[X]=3.5$, $\operatorname{var}(X)=35/12=2.916667$ (G2 §3). So $\mathbb{E}[S_{300}]=1050$, $\operatorname{var}(S_{300})=875$, $\sigma_{S_{300}}=29.580399$. The total is an integer, so widen to $[999.5,\,1100.5]$: $$z_{\text{lo}}=\frac{999.5-1050}{29.580399}=-1.707212,\qquad z_{\text{hi}}=\frac{1100.5-1050}{29.580399}=+1.707212,$$ and by symmetry $\Phi(1.707212)-\Phi(-1.707212)=2\Phi(1.707212)-1=0.912217$. The exact value, from a 300-fold convolution of the die PMF, is $0.912228$ — an error of $1.03\times10^{-5}$. Without the correction one gets $0.909031$, an error of $3.20\times10^{-3}$, i.e. $310$ times larger (keys pr_dice_ans, pr_dice_ans_plain, pr_dice_exact).

    2.5 Chebyshev versus the CLT: the same question, three answers

    Section 1 and this section now offer competing tools for the same tail probability. rec21 P1 is built to put them side by side on one question, so we do that first and draw the general lesson after.

    Example 2.6 (rec21 P1 — Markov, Chebyshev and the CLT on one question). Let $X_1,\dots,X_{10}$ be independent and uniformly distributed over $[0,1]$. Estimate $\mathbf{P}(X_1+\cdots+X_{10}\ge 7)$ using (a) the Markov inequality, (b) the Chebyshev inequality, (c) the central limit theorem.
    Solution (official solution, with all three [SOLUTION GAP]s filled)

    Common setup. Write $X=\sum_{i=1}^{10}X_i$. For a single uniform on $[0,1]$, $\mathbb{E}[X_i]=\tfrac12$ and $\operatorname{var}(X_i)=\tfrac1{12}$ (G3 §1). Hence, by linearity and by independence, $$\mathbb{E}[X]=10\times\tfrac12=5,\qquad \operatorname{var}(X)=10\times\tfrac1{12}=\tfrac{10}{12}=\tfrac56=0.833333,\qquad \sigma_X=\sqrt{5/6}=0.912871 .$$ [Gap filled: the official solution writes $\operatorname{var}(X)=10/12$ without deriving it.]

    (a) Markov. Markov's inequality (§1) requires only a nonnegative random variable and its mean: $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge a)\le\frac{\mathbb{E}[X]}{a}\quad\text{for } a>0 .$$ The $X_i$ are nonnegative, so $X\ge0$ and the hypothesis holds. With $a=7$: $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge 7)\le \frac{5}{7}=0.714286 .$$ The official solution prints $0.7142$. Interpretation: this says the event has probability at most $71\%$, which is true but useless — a probability is at most 1 anyway, and we have barely improved on that.

    (b) Chebyshev. Chebyshev's inequality bounds a two-sided deviation: $$\mathbf{P}(|X-\mathbb{E}[X]|\ge a)\le\frac{\operatorname{var}(X)}{a^2}.$$ Our event $\{X\ge7\}$ is $\{X-5\ge 2\}$, one side only, with $a=2$. The official solution's first line is $$2\,\mathbf{P}(X-5\ge2)=\mathbf{P}(|X-5|\ge2),$$ and it is used without comment. [Gap filled — why this is legitimate:] the distribution of $X$ is symmetric about its mean 5. Indeed, if $U$ is uniform on $[0,1]$ then so is $1-U$, so $(X_1,\dots,X_{10})$ and $(1-X_1,\dots,1-X_{10})$ have the same joint distribution; summing, $X$ and $10-X$ have the same distribution, i.e. $X-5$ and $-(X-5)$ have the same distribution. Hence the two tails are equal, $$\mathbf{P}(X-5\ge 2)=\mathbf{P}(X-5\le -2),$$ and since the two events are disjoint their union $\{|X-5|\ge2\}$ has exactly twice the probability. Now bound: $$2\,\mathbf{P}(X-5\ge 2)=\mathbf{P}(|X-5|\ge 2)\le\frac{\operatorname{var}(X)}{2^2} =\frac{10/12}{4}=\frac{10}{48}=0.208333,$$ so $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge7)\le\frac12\cdot\frac{10}{48}=\frac{10}{96}=\frac{5}{48}=0.104167 .$$ [Gap filled: the final arithmetic $\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac{10/12}{4}=\tfrac{10}{96}=\tfrac5{48}$ is not shown on the sheet.] The official answer is $0.1042$. Seven times better than Markov, and it used one extra number (the variance).

    (c) Central limit theorem. Now we approximate rather than bound. Start by flipping to the lower tail, since tables give $\Phi$: $$\mathbf{P}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{10}X_i\ge7\Big)=1-\mathbf{P}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{10}X_i\le 7\Big)$$ (the boundary point has probability 0 because $X$ is continuous). Standardize inside: $$\mathbf{P}\Big(\sum X_i\le 7\Big) =\mathbf{P}\!\left(\frac{\sum X_i-5}{\sqrt{10/12}}\le\frac{7-5}{\sqrt{10/12}}\right) \approx\Phi\!\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{10/12}}\right).$$ [Gap filled: the numerical evaluation is not shown.] Here $$\sqrt{10/12}=\sqrt{0.833333}=0.912871,\qquad \frac{2}{0.912871}=2.190890\approx 2.19,$$ and the table gives $\Phi(2.19)=0.985738$. Therefore $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge7)\approx 1-0.985738=0.014262,$$ the official $0.0143$. (With the unrounded $z=2.190890$: $1-\Phi(2.190890)=0.014230$.)

    The exact answer, for scoring. The sum of $n$ i.i.d. uniforms on $[0,1]$ has the Irwin–Hall distribution, whose CDF is $$\mathbf{P}(X\le x)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(x-k)^n .$$ Evaluated exactly with rational arithmetic at $n=10$, $x=7$ (key r1_exact_frac): $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge 7)=\frac{24427}{1814400}=0.013463 .$$ Now score the three tools against it:

    toolvalueratio to the truthwhat it used
    Markov$0.714286$$53.1\times$$\mathbb{E}[X]$ only, $X\ge0$
    Chebyshev (+ symmetry)$0.104167$$7.74\times$$\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, symmetry
    CLT$0.014230$$1.057\times$$\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, and $n$ largeish
    exact$\mathbf{0.013463}$$1\times$the full distribution
    The CLT is within $6\%$ of the truth at $n=10$ — because the uniform is symmetric, exactly as L20 slide 2 promised. Markov is off by a factor of 53. Figure 2.6 plots these same five numbers on a logarithmic axis, where the near-two-decade spread between Markov and the truth — and how narrowly the CLT misses it — is visible at a glance.

    A refinement worth knowing. Chebyshev + symmetry only worked because we happened to have a symmetric distribution. The one-sided Chebyshev (Cantelli) inequality needs no symmetry: $$\mathbf{P}(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\ge a)\le\frac{\operatorname{var}(X)}{\operatorname{var}(X)+a^2} =\frac{5/6}{5/6+4}=0.172414 .$$ Here it is worse than the symmetry route ($0.104167$), which is the usual trade: extra assumptions buy sharper bounds.

    Fig. 2.5 — Which tool for a tail probability (synthesizing §1, L20 slides 2 and 6, and B&T §5.4). Follow the arrows: the left branch produces guaranteed bounds from one or two moments; the right branch produces sharp approximations when $n$ is large; the bottom-right question decides whether the $1/2$ correction of §2.4 applies.
    Fig. 2.6 — rec21 P1 scored: five answers to one question, on a logarithmic axis. The dashed line is the exact value $24427/1814400=0.013463$. Markov overshoots by a factor of 53, Chebyshev by 7.7, and the CLT lands within $6\%$ — but only the first three are guaranteed to be upper bounds.
    Interpretation (what you are really trading). Markov, Chebyshev and Cantelli are bounds: whatever the distribution, the true probability is provably below them. The CLT is an approximation: it is usually far more accurate, but it comes with no guarantee at all and it can err on either side. Choose accordingly.
    • Need a certificate ("prove the failure probability is below $10^{-3}$")? Use a bound. An approximation is not a proof.
    • Need a number ("how many voters must I poll?")? Use the CLT — and note that its answer, being sharper, is smaller, which is exactly the direction that saves money.
    • Have only the mean? Markov is your only option. Have the variance too? Chebyshev. Have a large $n$? The CLT dominates both, by a lot.

    Polling, redux: the CLT versus Chebyshev on sample size

    Example 2.7 (L20 slide 4 — [UNSOLVED EXAMPLE]: the pollster's problem, finished; = B&T Example 5.11). Let $f$ be the (unknown) fraction of the population favoring a candidate. Poll $n$ people at random and set $$X_i=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if the } i\text{th person polled says yes,}\\ 0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases} \qquad M_n=\frac{X_1+\cdots+X_n}{n}.$$ How large must $n$ be so that $\mathbf{P}(|M_n-f|\ge 0.01)\le 0.05$? The slide sets the problem up, reduces it to a normal-tail statement, and stops — no numerical answer appears on any slide.
    Solution (the slide's chain completed, with its unstated step supplied)

    Step 1 — the model. The $X_i$ are i.i.d. Bernoulli$(f)$: each randomly selected person says yes with probability $f$, independently. So $\mathbb{E}[X_i]=f$ and $\sigma^2=f(1-f)$.

    Step 2 — rewrite the event as a statement about the standardized sum. The slide's algebra, line by line: $$|M_n-f|\ge 0.01 \iff \left|\frac{X_1+\cdots+X_n-nf}{n}\right|\ge 0.01 \iff \left|\frac{X_1+\cdots+X_n-nf}{\sqrt n\,\sigma}\right|\ge \frac{0.01\,n}{\sqrt n\,\sigma} =\frac{0.01\sqrt n}{\sigma},$$ where the second step multiplies both sides by $n/(\sqrt n\,\sigma)>0$, which preserves the inequality. The middle quantity is exactly $|Z_n|$.

    Step 3 — the CLT. Treating $Z_n$ as standard normal, $$\mathbf{P}(|M_n-f|\ge0.01)\approx\mathbf{P}\!\left(|Z|\ge\frac{0.01\sqrt n}{\sigma}\right).$$

    Step 4 — remove the unknown $\sigma$. The slide's next line replaces $0.01\sqrt n/\sigma$ by $0.02\sqrt n$ with no justification. [DERIVATION GAP filled:] for a Bernoulli variable $\sigma=\sqrt{f(1-f)}$, and the function $f\mapsto f(1-f)$ on $[0,1]$ is maximized at $f=1/2$ with value $1/4$ (its derivative $1-2f$ vanishes there), so $$\sigma=\sqrt{f(1-f)}\le\sqrt{1/4}=\tfrac12 \;\Longrightarrow\;\frac{0.01\sqrt n}{\sigma}\ge\frac{0.01\sqrt n}{1/2}=0.02\sqrt n .$$ A larger threshold means a smaller tail probability, so $$\mathbf{P}\!\left(|Z|\ge\frac{0.01\sqrt n}{\sigma}\right)\le\mathbf{P}\big(|Z|\ge0.02\sqrt n\big),$$ which is the slide's last line. We have converted an unknown into a worst case — Recipe step 6.

    Step 5 — impose the requirement and invert the table. By the symmetry of the normal density, $\mathbf{P}(|Z|\ge t)=2\big(1-\Phi(t)\big)$. So we need $$2\big(1-\Phi(0.02\sqrt n)\big)\le 0.05 \iff 1-\Phi(0.02\sqrt n)\le 0.025 \iff \Phi(0.02\sqrt n)\ge 0.975 .$$ Read the normal table backwards: $\Phi(1.96)=0.975002$, so the condition is $$0.02\sqrt n\ge 1.96 \iff \sqrt n\ge 98 \iff n\ge 98^2=9604 .$$ $$\boxed{n=9604 \text{ people suffice.}}$$ Check: $2\big(1-\Phi(0.02\sqrt{9604})\big)=2\big(1-\Phi(1.96)\big)=0.049996\le0.05$ ✓ (key poll_achieved). With the exact quantile $\Phi^{-1}(0.975)=1.959964$ the requirement is $n\ge 9603.647$, i.e. $n\ge 9604$ again.

    Compare with Chebyshev (§1). Chebyshev's route to the same guarantee gives $$\mathbf{P}(|M_n-f|\ge\epsilon)\le\frac{\operatorname{var}(M_n)}{\epsilon^2} =\frac{f(1-f)}{n\epsilon^2}\le\frac{1}{4n\epsilon^2},$$ so $\frac{1}{4n(0.01)^2}\le0.05$ forces $n\ge\frac{1}{4(0.01)^2(0.05)}=50{,}000$. The CLT needs $$\frac{50{,}000}{9604}=5.21\ \text{times fewer people}$$ (key poll_ratio) — and the factor $5.21$ is independent of $\epsilon$, as the table below shows. (It does depend on the confidence level: see Practice 2.10, where tightening $\delta$ from $0.05$ to $0.01$ pushes the same ratio to $15.1$.) B&T §5.4 (p. 277) reaches the same $9604$ versus $50{,}000$.

    accuracy $\epsilon$$n$ from the CLT$n$ from Chebyshevratio
    $0.05$$385$$2{,}000$$5.21$
    $0.03$$1{,}068$$5{,}556$$5.21$
    $0.02$$2{,}401$$12{,}500$$5.21$
    $0.01$$9{,}604$$50{,}000$$5.21$
    $0.005$$38{,}415$$200{,}000$$5.21$

    Why the ratio is constant. Both requirements have the form $n\ge \text{const}/\epsilon^2$: the CLT gives $n\ge (z^*)^2/(4\epsilon^2)$ with $z^*=1.959964$, Chebyshev gives $n\ge 1/(4\epsilon^2\delta)$ with $\delta=0.05$. The ratio is $1/\big(\delta (z^*)^2\big)=1/(0.05\times 3.8415)=5.21$, free of $\epsilon$. Both scale as $1/\epsilon^2$ — halving the margin of error quadruples the poll, whichever tool you use. What the CLT buys is the constant, not the scaling.

    Sanity check against reality. With $f=1/2$ (the worst case) and $n=9604$, the true binomial probability of $|M_n-f|\ge0.01$ is $0.048903$ (key poll_exact_binom_f0p5_n9604) — the CLT prediction $0.049996$ is within $0.0011$ of it and errs on the conservative side, whereas Chebyshev's guarantee at this $n$ would only have promised $\le 0.2603$ (key poll_cheb_guarantee_at_9604).

    Practice 2.9. Redo the first half of B&T Example 5.11: with $n=100$ and $\epsilon=0.1$, bound $\mathbf{P}(|M_{100}-f|\ge0.1)$ by the CLT, and compare with the Chebyshev answer from §1.
    Answer

    Worst-case $\sigma=1/2$, so the standardized threshold is $\epsilon\sqrt n/\sigma = 2\epsilon\sqrt n = 2\times0.1\times10=2$. Then $$\mathbf{P}(|M_{100}-f|\ge0.1)\approx 2\big(1-\Phi(2)\big)=2-2(0.977250)=0.045500 .$$ Using the book's two-decimal table value $\Phi(2)=0.977$: $2-2(0.977)=0.046$, exactly what B&T prints. Chebyshev gives $\frac{1}{4n\epsilon^2}=\frac{1}{4\times100\times0.01}=0.25$ — five and a half times larger, and "much smaller (and more accurate)" is the book's own comment. The exact binomial value at $f=1/2$ is $0.056888$ (key poll100_exact_p_half); note the CLT underestimates here, which is the price of an approximation with no guarantee — at $n=100$ with a lattice variable the residual error is a few percent.

    Practice 2.10. You want to estimate the mean of an Exponential$(1)$ population to within $\pm 0.05$ with probability $0.99$. How many samples does the CLT ask for, and how many does Chebyshev ask for?
    Answer

    Here $\sigma^2=1$, so $\operatorname{var}(M_n)=1/n$. CLT: we need $2\big(1-\Phi(0.05\sqrt n)\big)\le0.01$, i.e. $\Phi(0.05\sqrt n)\ge0.995$, i.e. $0.05\sqrt n\ge \Phi^{-1}(0.995)=2.575829$, i.e. $n\ge (2.575829/0.05)^2=2653.959$, so $n=2654$. Chebyshev: $\frac{1}{n(0.05)^2}\le0.01$ gives $n\ge 40{,}000$ (keys pr_size_n_ceil, pr_size_cheb) — a factor of $15.1$ more. The factor is larger than the polling factor $5.21$ because the confidence demand is stricter ($\delta=0.01$ rather than $0.05$), and the ratio $1/(\delta(z^*)^2)$ grows as $\delta$ shrinks. Caveat: the exponential is skewed, so at $n=2654$ the CLT answer is trustworthy, but a CLT sample-size calculation for $n$ in the dozens would not be.

    2.6 Two consequences: Stirling's formula, and where the CLT must not go

    The last two items on the syllabus are a triumph and a trap: the CLT producing a famous formula about factorials, and the CLT apparently proving something absurd about the Poisson distribution.

    Example 2.8 (rec21 P3 — sums of unit-mean Poissons and Stirling's formula). Let $X_1,X_2,\dots$ be independent Poisson random variables with mean and variance $1$, and let $S_n=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i$.
    (a) Show that $S_n$ is Poisson with mean and variance $n$.
    (b) Show how the CLT suggests $n!\approx\sqrt{2\pi n}\,(n/e)^n$ for large $n$.
    Solution (official solution, with the [SOLUTION GAP] filled)

    (a) The Poisson-process argument (official solution). Interpret $X_i$ as the number of arrivals in the $i$th interval of length 1 in a Poisson process of rate $\lambda=1$ (G4 §3): the number of arrivals in an interval of length $\tau$ is Poisson with parameter $\lambda\tau=\tau$, so each $X_i$ is indeed Poisson with mean 1, and arrivals in disjoint intervals are independent, so the $X_i$ are independent. Laying the $n$ intervals end to end, $S_n=X_1+\cdots+X_n$ counts the arrivals in one interval of length $n$. Therefore $S_n$ is Poisson with parameter $n$: $$\mathbf{P}(S_n=k)=e^{-n}\frac{n^k}{k!},\qquad \mathbb{E}[S_n]=\operatorname{var}(S_n)=n .$$ (One can also prove this by convolution or transforms, but the process argument is one line.)

    (b) Stirling from the CLT. The plan: compute $\mathbf{P}(S_n=n)$ — the probability that the Poisson sum lands exactly on its own mean — in two ways, exactly and by the CLT, and equate.

    Way 1: exactly. Put $k=n$ in the Poisson PMF from part (a): $$\mathbf{P}(S_n=n)=e^{-n}\frac{n^n}{n!} .$$

    Way 2: by the CLT with the $1/2$ correction. Since $\mathbb{E}[S_n]=n$ and $\sigma_{S_n}=\sqrt n$, the standardized sum is $Z_n=(S_n-n)/\sqrt n$. Now, step by step: $$\mathbf{P}(S_n=n)=\mathbf{P}\Big(n-\tfrac12<S_n<n+\tfrac12\Big),$$ which is exact because $S_n$ is integer-valued — this is the $1/2$ correction of §2.4, and it is the reason the whole derivation works. Standardize by subtracting $n$ and dividing by $\sqrt n$: $$=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\frac{-1}{2\sqrt n}<\frac{S_n-n}{\sqrt n}\le\frac{1}{2\sqrt n}\right) \approx\mathbf{P}\!\left(\frac{-1}{2\sqrt n}<Z\le\frac{1}{2\sqrt n}\right),$$ the approximation being the CLT (with $Z$ standard normal). Write the last probability as an integral of the standard normal density: $$=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-1/(2\sqrt n)}^{1/(2\sqrt n)}e^{-z^2/2}\,dz .$$ [Gap filled — the "second approximation":] the interval of integration has length $$\frac{1}{2\sqrt n}-\Big(\frac{-1}{2\sqrt n}\Big)=\frac{1}{\sqrt n},$$ and it shrinks to zero as $n$ grows, so by the mean-value form of the fundamental theorem of calculus the integral equals (length) $\times$ (integrand at some interior point), and that interior point may be taken to be $z=0$ in the limit. Since $e^{-z^2/2}\big|_{z=0}=e^0=1$, $$\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt n}\cdot 1=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi n}} .$$

    Equate the two ways. $$e^{-n}\frac{n^n}{n!}\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi n}} \;\Longrightarrow\; n!\approx n^n e^{-n}\sqrt{2\pi n}=\boxed{\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^{n}},$$ which is Stirling's formula.

    How good is it, numerically (key stirling)?

    $n$$n!$$\sqrt{2\pi n}(n/e)^n$relative error$\lambda_n=\ln\!\big(n!\big)-\ln(\text{approx})$bounds $\big(\tfrac1{12n+1},\tfrac1{12n}\big)$
    $1$$1$$0.922137$$-7.79\%$$0.081061$$(0.076923,\,0.083333)$
    $5$$120$$118.019168$$-1.65\%$$0.016645$$(0.016393,\,0.016667)$
    $10$$3\,628\,800$$3\,598\,696$$-0.83\%$$0.008331$$(0.008264,\,0.008333)$
    $50$$3.0414\times10^{64}$$3.0363\times10^{64}$$-0.17\%$$0.0016666$$(0.0016639,\,0.0016667)$
    $100$$9.3326\times10^{157}$$9.3248\times10^{157}$$-0.083\%$$0.00083333$$(0.00083264,\,0.00083333)$
    Even at $n=1$ the formula is within $8\%$. The last two columns verify the sharper statement quoted at the end of the official solution, $n!=n^ne^{-n}\sqrt{2\pi n}\,e^{\lambda_n}$ with $\frac{1}{12n+1}<\lambda_n<\frac{1}{12n}$: every computed $\lambda_n$ lies inside its bracket. As the solution sheet says, those refined bounds cannot be obtained from the CLT — the CLT gives the leading behavior, nothing more.

    Where the CLT step is doing the work. Check the two approximations separately (key poisson_atom). At $n=100$: the exact $\mathbf{P}(S_n=n)=0.03986100$; the CLT interval $\Phi(0.05)-\Phi(-0.05)=0.03987761$ (error $+0.04\%$, this is the CLT); and $1/\sqrt{2\pi n}=0.03989423$ (error $+0.083\%$, this adds the mean-value step). Both approximations are individually excellent, and their errors do not cancel — they are the $0.083\%$ in the table.

    Historical footnote (from the solution sheet). De Moivre first found the form $n!\approx n^{n+1/2}e^{-n}\cdot(\text{constant})$ with a complicated expression for the constant; his friend Stirling identified the constant as $\sqrt{2\pi}$.

    Interpretation (why this derivation is remarkable). Stirling's formula is a statement about factorials — pure combinatorics, no randomness anywhere. The CLT proves it by inventing a random variable whose PMF at one point happens to contain $n!$, and then reading that PMF off the normal curve. The $\sqrt{2\pi}$ in Stirling's formula is literally the $\sqrt{2\pi}$ in the normal density. Note also which ingredient carries the content: without the $1/2$ correction, the CLT would have said $\mathbf{P}(S_n=n)\approx 0$ and the derivation would have produced nothing.
    Example 2.9 (L20 slide 8 — the Poisson-versus-normal paradox, resolved). The slide poses an argument decorated with "(??)" and "(????)": Poisson arrivals during a unit interval equal the sum of $n$ independent Poisson counts over $n$ intervals of length $1/n$. Let $n\to\infty$ and apply the CLT (??). Poisson = normal (????). What is wrong?
    Solution (the resolution the lecturer gives orally; the slide writes only the question marks)

    The argument, stated carefully. Fix a Poisson process of rate $\lambda=1$ and let $N$ be the number of arrivals in $[0,1]$, so $N\sim$ Poisson$(1)$. Split $[0,1]$ into $n$ subintervals of length $1/n$ and let $Y_i^{(n)}$ be the count in the $i$th. These are independent, identically distributed (each Poisson with parameter $1/n$), and $N=Y_1^{(n)}+\cdots+Y_n^{(n)}$ exactly. So $N$ is an i.i.d. sum of $n$ terms for every $n$. Letting $n\to\infty$ and "applying the CLT" would say $N$ is normal — but $N$ is Poisson$(1)$, a discrete variable taking the values $0,1,2,\dots$, which is certainly not normal.

    The error. The CLT is a statement about a fixed sequence $X_1,X_2,\dots$ whose common distribution does not depend on $n$; the limit $n\to\infty$ adds new terms drawn from that same fixed distribution. In the argument above, the summands' distribution changes with $n$: the $i$th summand is Poisson$(1/n)$, and as $n$ grows each summand becomes more and more degenerate (it is $0$ with probability $e^{-1/n}\to1$). We are not adding more copies of one distribution; we are re-splitting one fixed random variable into ever finer pieces, whose sum is the same $N$ every time. [DERIVATION GAP filled: the slide marks the flaw with "(??)" but never writes the resolution.] Formally, the standardized sum here is $$\frac{N-n\cdot(1/n)}{\sqrt{n}\cdot\sqrt{1/n}}=\frac{N-1}{1},$$ which does not depend on $n$ at all — there is no limit being taken, and nothing converges to anything. The CLT hypothesis "i.i.d., distribution fixed as $n\to\infty$" is doing real work.

    The correct picture — two different limits of the binomial (L20 slide 8). Both approximations to Binomial$(n,p)$ are limits, but along different paths:

    • $p$ fixed, $n\to\infty$: normal. Each summand keeps a fixed distribution, so the CLT applies. The variance $np(1-p)\to\infty$ and the distribution spreads over $\sqrt{n}$ values.
    • $np=\mu$ fixed, $n\to\infty$, $p\to0$: Poisson. The summands' distribution changes with $n$ (they become rarer and rarer), so the CLT does not apply; the Poisson limit of G4 §3 applies instead. The variance stays bounded at $np(1-p)\to\mu$.
    The slide's two examples make the choice concrete: with $p=1/100$, $n=100$ use Poisson ($\mu=1$); with $p=1/10$, $n=500$ use normal ($np=50$, $\sigma=6.708204$). The numbers confirm it (key poisson_vs_normal): for $n=100$, $p=0.01$ the largest PMF error is $0.001850$ for the Poisson approximation and $0.124215$ for the normal one — the normal is off by a factor of $67$, because it cannot reproduce the huge atom $\mathbf{P}(S=0)=0.366$. For $n=500$, $p=0.1$ the largest CDF error is $0.013335$ for Poisson and $0.007906$ for normal, and the normal now wins.

    Rule of thumb.
